It hasn’t just been one odd day. It’s been going on long enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing anymore.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months, have you had a period where you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric for days at a time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I guess. I mean, my job can be intense, and there are days where I’m running on adrenaline and coffee and trying to act like everything is fine, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt unusually euphoric or like I was on top of the world for days.\n\nIf anything, I’ve felt keyed up because I’m trying to keep myself together and not look unstable in front of people. But “high” or energized in a good way… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months, have you had a period of feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed most of the day nearly every day. I mean, I’ve definitely been upset and worn down by all of this, and there are days where I feel embarrassed or scared or just exhausted from trying to function normally. But “depressed” feels like… I don’t know, maybe a specific thing, and I’m not sure that’s what’s been happening.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking my mood that way. Some days I’m anxious and tense, some days I’m angry at myself for not being able to handle things better, and some days I sort of just push through because there isn’t really another option. But I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a stretch where I’m depressed all day, almost every day. But an actual plot against me, like people organizing to hurt me… I can’t say I’ve felt certain of that. At home, my husband has had to manage most household responsibilities because I leave things unfinished or do not start them at all.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… noticeably is almost an understatement, honestly. I’ve always been the person people came to when something needed a decision, or when things were messy and someone had to make a call. That was my job, really. But since all this began, I’m not reliably doing that anymore.

I still show up, and I try to look like I’m managing, but I’m not keeping up with the basic executive decisions the way I used to. Things sit on my desk, or in my inbox, or I keep rereading the same material and not moving it forward. Senior staff have started quietly taking over parts of my work—things I would never have handed off before. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. Meetings get redirected around me, someone else summarizes, someone else follows up, someone else makes sure the decision actually happens.

And at home it’s similar, maybe worse because I can’t hide it as well. My husband has had to take on most of the household responsibilities. I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I either don’t start it at all or I start and leave it half done. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m used to being competent, but yes, my ability to work has dropped a lot.

Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because it’s not how I used to be at all.

I used to be very verbal, probably too verbal. At work I was the one driving the discussion, pushing ideas forward, calling people after openings or meetings, following up immediately, getting energy from it. I could talk through a problem for an hour and feel more alive afterward. Now I can sit at my desk for hours just staring at an agenda, knowing I’m supposed to say something or move the room toward a decision, and I just… don’t. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but I can’t make myself care enough to pull them out.

And at home, too. My husband will ask me normal things, simple things, and I’ll answer with a few words or not really answer at all. It isn’t that I’m angry at him. I’m not trying to punish anyone. I just feel sort of emptied out. Flat. Like everything has the same weight, or no weight.

The strange part is that it includes things I used to love about my work. I used to feel proud or excited or stressed in a good way, like it mattered. Now sometimes I look at something that would have completely engaged me before, and I feel almost nothing. Just blank. So yes, I’m talking less, and I feel much less emotionally connected than I normally would.

  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, yes, definitely longer than a month.

The voices—the ones that seem like they’re coming from the walls sometimes, or from my phone even when nothing is actually playing—those have been happening for about three months now. It’s not like it was just one bad week and then it stopped. I wish it had been that. It’s been this ongoing thing, and even when I try to tell myself it’s stress or I’m mishearing something, it keeps happening.

So yes, there has been at least a full month where they were still present. More like the past three months, on and off but continuing through that whole period.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I don’t know how else to say it, but I keep hearing people talking about me. Like through the conference room walls, or sometimes from my phone even when it’s off, and it’s not vague. It sounds clear. It sounds like board members, staff—people I know—saying I’m ruining the company, that I’m not fit to be there, things like that.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds, I do, but in the moment it feels completely real. I’ll actually answer them, like out loud, before I catch myself and realize nobody else in the room heard anything. Then I look around and people are staring at me, or they’re confused, and I feel… humiliated, honestly. It’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off anymore, and it’s affecting me at work.\n\nDoctor: How long has this been happening?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three months, I think. The voices from the walls, or sometimes from my phone, started happening around then. At first I tried to explain it away, like maybe I was overhearing something or maybe the phone had glitched somehow, but it’s kept happening over these last few months. It hasn’t just been one odd day. It’s been going on long enough that I can’t pretend it’s nothing anymore.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months, have you had a period where you felt unusually high, energized, or euphoric for days at a time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I guess. I mean, my job can be intense, and there are days where I’m running on adrenaline and coffee and trying to act like everything is fine, but I wouldn’t say I’ve felt unusually euphoric or like I was on top of the world for days.\n\nIf anything, I’ve felt keyed up because I’m trying to keep myself together and not look unstable in front of people. But “high” or energized in a good way… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: During these past three months, have you had a period of feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed most of the day nearly every day. I mean, I’ve definitely been upset and worn down by all of this, and there are days where I feel embarrassed or scared or just exhausted from trying to function normally. But “depressed” feels like… I don’t know, maybe a specific thing, and I’m not sure that’s what’s been happening.\n\nI haven’t really been tracking my mood that way. Some days I’m anxious and tense, some days I’m angry at myself for not being able to handle things better, and some days I sort of just push through because there isn’t really another option. But I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a stretch where I’m depressed all day, almost every day. At least not that I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Before these voices started, were you diagnosed with any medical or neurological illness?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I mean, I’ve had the normal sort of things people have over the years, checkups and headaches here and there, getting run down from work, that kind of thing, but I was never told I had some neurological condition or serious medical illness before this started.\n\nUnless you mean something very specific that I wouldn’t think to count? But no, there wasn’t some diagnosis beforehand that I can point to and say, “Oh, that explains it.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sure that people are deliberately trying to harm you or plot against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I feel sure of that. “Deliberately trying to harm me” sounds very… definite, and I don’t want to exaggerate or say something that makes it sound worse than it is.\n\nI do get worried about people’s intentions sometimes, especially in situations where I’m already stressed, and I can replay things people said and wonder what they really meant by it. But an actual plot against me, like people organizing to hurt me… I can’t say I’ve felt certain of that. I haven’t noticed something that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: Since these experiences began, has your ability to do your work dropped noticeably?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… noticeably is almost an understatement, honestly. I’ve always been the person people came to when something needed a decision, or when things were messy and someone had to make a call. That was my job, really. But since all this began, I’m not reliably doing that anymore.\n\nI still show up, and I try to look like I’m managing, but I’m not keeping up with the basic executive decisions the way I used to. Things sit on my desk, or in my inbox, or I keep rereading the same material and not moving it forward. Senior staff have started quietly taking over parts of my work—things I would never have handed off before. They don’t always say it directly, but I can tell. Meetings get redirected around me, someone else summarizes, someone else follows up, someone else makes sure the decision actually happens.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar, maybe worse because I can’t hide it as well. My husband has had to take on most of the household responsibilities. I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I either don’t start it at all or I start and leave it half done. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m used to being competent, but yes, my ability to work has dropped a lot.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you found yourself talking much less than usual or feeling emotionally flat compared with how you normally are?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because it’s not how I used to be at all.\n\nI used to be very verbal, probably too verbal. At work I was the one driving the discussion, pushing ideas forward, calling people after openings or meetings, following up immediately, getting energy from it. I could talk through a problem for an hour and feel more alive afterward. Now I can sit at my desk for hours just staring at an agenda, knowing I’m supposed to say something or move the room toward a decision, and I just… don’t. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but I can’t make myself care enough to pull them out.\n\nAnd at home, too. My husband will ask me normal things, simple things, and I’ll answer with a few words or not really answer at all. It isn’t that I’m angry at him. I’m not trying to punish anyone. I just feel sort of emptied out. Flat. Like everything has the same weight, or no weight.\n\nThe strange part is that it includes things I used to love about my work. I used to feel proud or excited or stressed in a good way, like it mattered. Now sometimes I look at something that would have completely engaged me before, and I feel almost nothing. Just blank. So yes, I’m talking less, and I feel much less emotionally connected than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: At any point since this began, have the voices been present continuously for at least one full month?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, definitely longer than a month.\n\nThe voices—the ones that seem like they’re coming from the walls sometimes, or from my phone even when nothing is actually playing—those have been happening for about three months now. It’s not like it was just one bad week and then it stopped. I wish it had been that. It’s been this ongoing thing, and even when I try to tell myself it’s stress or I’m mishearing something, it keeps happening.\n\nSo yes, there has been at least a full month where they were still present. My husband will ask me normal things, simple things, and I’ll answer with a few words or not really answer at all. It isn’t that I’m angry at him. I’m not trying to punish anyone. I just feel sort of emptied out. Flat. Like everything has the same weight, or no weight.\n\nThe strange part is that it includes things I used to love about my work. I used to feel proud or excited or stressed in a good way, like it mattered. Now sometimes I look at something that would have completely engaged me before, and I feel almost nothing. Just blank. So yes, I’m talking less, and I feel much less emotionally connected than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: At any point since this began, have the voices been present continuously for at least one full month?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, definitely longer than a month.\n\nThe voices—the ones that seem like they’re coming from the walls sometimes, or from my phone even when nothing is actually playing—those have been happening for about three months now. It’s not like it was just one bad week and then it stopped. I wish it had been that. It’s been this ongoing thing, and even when I try to tell myself it’s stress or I’m mishearing something, it keeps happening.\n\nSo yes, there has been at least a full month where they were still present. More like the past three months, on and off but continuing through that whole period.\n\n[Last Question]\nFrom the very first time these experiences began, has it been at least six months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 43 / chief_executive / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been building for about 2 years and 4 months, since the company expansion put me in charge of the new arts-and-commerce division.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It’s been more than six months. A lot more, really.

If I go back to the very beginning—not just when it became impossible to ignore, but when I first started feeling like something was… off—it started around the company expansion, when they put me in charge of the new arts-and-commerce division. That was about two years and four months ago.

At the time I kept telling myself it was just the pressure of the new role, because there was so much to build and so many people watching me. But if you’re asking from the first time these experiences began, then yes, it’s been well over six months. Around two years and four months.

---------- turn 1 | judge:symptom_extraction | symptom_diagnosis.identify_symptoms_turn() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [judge] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a clinical psychiatrist. Output only valid JSON."
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "You are a clinical psychiatrist reviewing a single patient utterance.\n\n=== SYMPTOM CATALOGUE ===\n[S001] Detail_Inattention_Errors: Often fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes.\n[S002] Sustained_Attention_Difficulty: Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\n[S003] Listening_Difficulty: Often does not seem to listen when spoken to directly.\n[S004] Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n[S005] Organization_Difficulty: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n[S006] Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n[S007] Losing_Items: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n[S008] Distractibility: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n[S009] Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n[S010] Fidgeting: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n[S011] Leaving_Seat: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n[S012] Feeling_Restless: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.  Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. I’ll actually answer them, like out loud, before I catch myself and realize nobody else in the room heard anything. Then I look around and people are staring at me, or they’re confused, and I feel… humiliated, honestly. It’s been happening enough that I can’t just brush it off anymore, and it’s affecting me at work.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt’s been… about three months, I think. The voices from the walls, or sometimes from my phone, started happening around then. It hasn’t just been one odd day.   }

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
